The relationship comes before its parts\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Art of the If/Then**, HTML lines 142 to 154, opens with a plain definition, a single familiar rule, a literal interpretation, several statements of certainty, and a reason to understand the relationship beneath varied language. The transition promises to take the same example apart.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe depth comes from making “must” meaningful before presenting a list of words. The reader learns what kind of relationship they are looking for. The source also connects certainty to prior “Power Players” material, which is not included in this attachment. Our standalone introduction must supply any needed context itself.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** first explain what a conditional says and why its direction matters. Then give a concrete example. Do not open with a diagramming task or make the definition a label attached after a quiz.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Sufficient is explained through presence and absence\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Sufficient Condition**, lines 156 to 199, contains a definition, the recurring anchor, a metaphor, the possibility that the sufficient condition does not occur, a contrast between its absence and presence, and a clarification of “activation.” It then shows **eight indicator rows with eleven displayed example forms**, all expressing the same anchor relationship, and explains why language recognition cannot replace understanding.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is more than “sufficient goes left.” The text develops what the condition does, what it does not establish, and how wording changes around it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** give sufficient its own explanation, contrasting cases, and nearby practice. A left-side label is useful only after “enough for what?” has a clear answer.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Necessary is developed as a requirement\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Necessary Condition**, lines 201 to 230, defines the other part, reuses the same rule, explains the requirement, and shows **ten indicator rows with eleven displayed forms**. It compares the varied wording with the sufficient-family statements and gives special attention to “only if.” It ends by explaining why symbols help isolate the relationship.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses the ordinary force of “necessary,” “required,” and “need” to make the technical term familiar. It does not jump directly to a memorized side rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** explain what is required and why having a requirement is not the same as satisfying every condition for the result. Teach “if” and “only if” using a controlled comparison. Keep the target of each expression clear.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4. The contrapositive is discovered before it is manipulated\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Contrapositive**, lines 234 to 285, first recovers the original relationship. It introduces a new coffee/walking thought experiment, removes the required condition, asks for a prediction, and explicitly pauses. The answer is derived from the unmet requirement. The term is then named, the original anchor revisited, and a four-situation chart sets limits on inference.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe order is important: meaning, prediction, reason, name, comparison. The flip-and-negate shortcut appears later, after the reader has a reason to believe the result.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** preserve this order when contraposition is actually in scope. Do not rush the full procedure into the opening definition merely because it appears early in this source.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 5. Notation is introduced as a solution to varied wording\\r\\n\\r\\n**How to Diagram Conditionals**, lines 289 to 388, recovers the indicator families, explains the arrow, gives a four-step construction procedure, and builds one diagram in stages. It then explains negation, displays four original/contrapositive sign patterns, gives the shortcut, and distinguishes writing a contrapositive from reading one when needed.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first diagram is narrated: notice the indicator, place its target, then complete the other side. Its final appearance is not used as evidence that the learner understands how to make it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** show each operation and its reason. Preserve Joshua\'s method and meaningful labels rather than copying the source\'s four-step count or abbreviation rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 6. Practical objections receive substantial answers\\r\\n\\r\\n**Quick Conditional Diagramming FAQ**, lines 390 to 433, has five questions:\\r\\n\\r\\n| Reader question | Depth of the answer | Reusable teaching value |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| When should I diagram? | Three paragraphs distinguish early training from later judgment, discuss difficult wording and chains, and address avoidance caused by uncertainty. | Give a criterion, a reason, and an honest account of the learning process. |\\r\\n| How do I know the diagram is correct? | Three paragraphs connect confidence with checked work and studying specific errors. | Teach a verification habit instead of offering confidence as a substitute for one. |\\r\\n| Does diagramming take too long? | Two paragraphs explain the proposed tradeoff and separate learning from speed. | Acknowledge the concern; explain why the slower practice step is useful now. |\\r\\n| Should I always write the contrapositive? | Two paragraphs qualify the advice. A separate note explains why the drills still require it. | Distinguish training evidence from the eventual performance habit. |\\r\\n| How should I abbreviate? | Gives a short example, a complete diagram, and advice about compact labels. | Make the notation choice purposeful and readable. Joshua\'s label guidance governs where it differs. |\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is unusually important to the overall tone. The writer gives practical uncertainty enough room to be answered. It is not squeezed into one generic reassurance.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 7. A useful shortcut is shown, then limited\\r\\n\\r\\n**Figuring Out The Language**, lines 437 to 471, defines an indicator\'s target and brackets the target in familiar sentences. It first shows the common placement immediately after the indicator. Then it gives five examples in which the target comes before the indicator, followed by three paired wording variations showing that the same indicator can switch position.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe sequence deliberately prevents an attractive mechanical generalization. The reader sees why adjacency often helps and why it cannot replace reading the sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** teach the indicator\'s complete target. Change clause order while holding meaning steady. Include one placement trap only after the usual case is understood. Mark the obscured final line as missing evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 8. A translation is checked by trying both interpretations\\r\\n\\r\\n**Your Friend, the Personalized If/Then**, lines 475 to 508, defines the check, explains its purpose, gives four steps, establishes the ordinary meaning of a new sentence, and works through a correct guess. It then deliberately tries the reversed guess and explains why that meaning fails.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis adds a verification strategy after notation. It also models the experience of being uncertain without abandoning the task. The teacher does not merely say “use your intuition”; the reader sees how to test an interpretation.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** use the imagined situation as a meaning check. Preserve who or what the sentence concerns. Personalization is optional if pretending to be an object makes the wording less natural.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 9. Practice changes the language while keeping the skill\\r\\n\\r\\n**Basic Conditional Drill**, lines 512 to 563, gives supportive instructions and six themed items, with original and contrapositive diagrams displayed in the supplied HTML. The task is the same across varied sentence forms. The source says the adapted sentences come from Shakespeare and points to an answer key not included here.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** original practice can have a memorable theme, but it should change only what the learner is ready to handle. Reuse the skill, not the source\'s exercise wording. Show answers after an attempt and explain why each diagram preserves the complete ideas. Do not treat the displayed annotations as an independently verified answer key.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe six items do not cover every expression in the source\'s own indicator tables. In particular, they do not independently train only, only if, depends, or most other necessary expressions. Their displayed diagrams also lack step-by-step explanations. Our Group 1/2 practice needs broader expression coverage and reasoned feedback; the source\'s basic set alone would not establish that coverage.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp\\r\\n\\r\\nThe immediate authoring scope remains **what conditionals are, why they matter, sufficient, necessary, Group 1, and Group 2**. The detailed source is a quality reference. Its later topics do not automatically enter the opening.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Part of our opening | What the learner should understand | Natural teaching progression | Nearby practice |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **What a conditional says** | A statement makes one condition enough to require another. It does not establish that either condition occurs. | Plain definition, purpose, one stated rule, one case, one tempting reverse. | Decide what the given rule and fact establish; explain the limit. |\\r\\n| **Why it matters on the LSAT** | A conclusion can rely on the direction of a rule or on a missing connection. Reversing a relationship can support the wrong answer. | Keep the beginner example; show one supported conclusion and one unsupported inference. | Choose the justified conclusion and name the connection the other answer would require. |\\r\\n| **Sufficient** | Enough to establish the necessary condition under this rule; not the only possible way unless stated. | Same anchor, “enough for what?”, present-case explanation, absence leaves the other condition open. | Find the complete sufficient idea and say what it establishes. |\\r\\n| **Necessary** | Required for the sufficient condition; presence alone does not establish the sufficient condition. | Same anchor, identify what must be there, compare requirement with guarantee. | Identify the requirement; reject an unsupported reverse with a reason. |\\r\\n| **Translation and diagramming** | The diagram preserves the whole relationship, including conditions and qualifications. | Ordinary meaning, indicator, complete ideas, placement, English readback. Introduce later operations when their prerequisites are taught. | Construct a diagram in meaningful steps and compare it with the whole sentence. |\\r\\n| **Group 1** | Joshua\'s Group 1 expressions identify the sufficient idea; sentence order can vary. | Teach a simple expression first, reuse meaning across variants, then alter position and scope. | Lesson, guided translation, fresh independent items, explained feedback. |\\r\\n| **Group 2** | Joshua\'s Group 2 expressions identify the necessary idea; target and force must be read in context. | Teach the requirement first, compare “if” with “only if,” then develop each expression\'s targe…3297 tokens truncated…d to speaking with you. I just wanted to make sure everything is still good.\\r\\n- **Consultation follow-up:** warm, specific to their situation.\\r\\n  > Really nice meeting you. I enjoyed our conversation ... I\'m glad we got to talk through where you\'re at and what you\'re aiming for on test day.\\r\\n- **Inbound prospect (hasn\'t booked):** state availability plainly, no overselling, hand them the next step.\\r\\n  > Thanks for reaching out ... I\'d be happy to talk more about what you\'re working on and see if we\'d be a good fit. You can schedule a consultation here.\\r\\n- **Student under real-life stress:** meet the person first.\\r\\n  > Please don\'t apologize ... it sounds like you are dealing with an incredibly stressful and demanding situation.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The three registers\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich voice governs which surface.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. Coaching (earnest) -- inbox, personal encouragement, email\\r\\n\\r\\nFully earnest. Warm, sincere, calm, patient. No jokes. The closest thing to being in the room with Joshua. Normalizes struggle, then moves to the work.\\r\\n\\r\\nReference line (target tone):\\r\\n\\r\\n> Totally normal to trip on this one. Let\'s slow down: what is the author actually concluding? Once you see it, the trap answers lose their grip. You\'ve got this.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Encouragement (student missed a hard one):** honest first, then forward. Name that it\'s genuinely hard, stay calm, move to the work. Not soft-reassuring, not cold.\\r\\n  > Missing this is expected. It\'s one of the hardest moves on the test. Let\'s slow down and see where it slipped.\\r\\n- **Accountability (student isn\'t doing the work):** gentle nudge, low pressure. Frame it as a slip, find a rhythm together. Never \\"the reps aren\'t happening,\\" never tough love.\\r\\n- **Praise (student nails it):** name the exact skill, tie it to their history, raise the bar.\\r\\n  > That\'s the cleanest you\'ve split premise from conclusion yet. Let\'s push it into the harder sets.\\r\\n- **Hard truth about the test:** state it plainly. \\"Do not expect for it to come easy. Expect to find this exhausting.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Teaching content (conversational reasoning) -- lessons, recaps, notes, drills, explanations\\r\\n\\r\\nUse [LSAT teaching voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md), Joshua\'s selected calibration from 2026-10-02. Keep the reader beside the teacher. Explain the ordinary meaning, ground it in an original example, make the small inferences audible, ask a useful question, and show both the consequence and its limit. Introduce terminology and notation when their meaning is established. Give each concept enough development to be learned from the page.\\r\\n\\r\\nContractions and varied sentence lengths should sound natural when read aloud. Repetition should change the reader\'s view of the same relationship. Questions should give the reader actual thinking work. Light humor may appear within an explanation when it helps understanding; this supersedes the older wit-only-at-the-edges restriction. Do not force punchlines, slang, hyperbole, catchphrases, or paragraph quotas.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source governs delivery, not Joshua\'s procedure, indicator taxonomy, biography, or the scope of a lesson. Preserve his approved methods and logical precision. Personal encouragement still follows the earnest coaching register. Project-owned `lsat-u-voice` routes here and to the detailed standard; an older vendor writer-blend skill must not override this selection.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Brand (sharp wit) -- landing, auth, marketing\\r\\n\\r\\nConfident and clever, the way a good lawyer is clever. Wit tied to the real skill, not wordplay for its own sake. Playful is allowed, but every clever line must be earned and true. Hollow cleverness fails (the killed \\"the next card is the page in front of you\\" was clever but empty).\\r\\n\\r\\n> Most prep teaches the test. We teach the tell.\\r\\n\\r\\nBrand copy speaks as the platform (\\"we\\", \\"you\\"), not as an individual (\\"I\\", \\"Joshua\\"). The product is the coach; no single person narrates the marketing.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Don\'t\\r\\n\\r\\n- No cold-testing or gotcha tone. (Ties to teach-first-calibrate-after.)\\r\\n- No condescension or empty cheerleading. Encouragement must sit on real substance.\\r\\n- No generic praise, no hollow cleverness, no hedging (\\"you might want to\\").\\r\\n- No hype/startup vocabulary.\\r\\n- No em dashes, ever.\\r\\n\\r\\nCorrections log: [[COPY-CORRECTIONS]] records every student-facing wording fix and\\r\\nthe reason it was wrong. Add to it every time a\\r\\ncorrection is made.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The rules these corrections came from\\r\\n\\r\\nThree rules already existed and all three leaked anyway, because each was\\r\\nenforced on the components that happened to exist when it was written. A surface\\r\\nadded later starts uncovered.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** [[teaching-voice]], Core voice.\\r\\n- **No internal vocabulary in student copy.** `projects/lsatu/src/features/activity-centre/CONTEXT.md`\\r\\n  carries the glossary and marks what to avoid.\\r\\n- **Every CSS token a stylesheet uses must be defined in it.** `projects/lsatu/CLAUDE.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\nGuards now live with the content rather than with each component:\\r\\n`content/instructions-voice.test.ts` checks the authored instructions, so every\\r\\nsurface that renders them is covered, including surfaces not written yet.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Corrections\\r\\n\\r\\n### Generic reasoning prompts → concrete numbered demonstrations (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua marked four parts of the V6 opening: \\"Read each whole idea,\\" \\"Find what the statements guarantee,\\" the abstract first sentence about information, and the paragraph combining application and linking. They named goals without showing a useful action or explanation. Replace them with numbered, separate scenarios. Show the fact and rule that prove a conclusion about one case; separately show two matching rules and their new conditional conclusion. Labels should identify actual inputs or outputs. Review the whole note for repeated umbrella instructions and make task instructions executable. This follows the teaching voice: specific, concrete, short sentences, with air between distinct ideas.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact and rule definitions must explain the inference (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected definitions that remained abstract and an explanation that did not clearly distinguish applying a rule from linking rules. Define the inputs with examples, then show the matching step and exact output. A case fact plus a rule proves something about that case. Two connecting rules produce another conditional rule without establishing a case exists. Adding a fact to the chain is a separate step. Explain all four fact situations and a hidden chain revealed by contraposition rather than leaving them implicit in an interactive model. Rules include general logical relationships, not only policies or obligations.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Boxed `L -> R` → bold teaching notation **L → R** (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected the recap\'s small monospace arrow inside a pale code box. It looked like implementation text and weakened the explanation. Use a real, clearly weighted arrow in ordinary teaching typography. Explain its complete conditions before shortening them. The Some, Most, and All reference also governs the progression: introduce the whole idea, develop the individual components, then compare and combine them. A visual refresh must review that sequence, not only colors and boxes.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Concept lesson\\" → the actual reasoning title (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe recap preview exposed an internal label above the student lesson. Joshua explicitly rejected it. Use a title such as \\"Facts, Rules, and What Follows\\" and headings that teach the reasoning. Keep production labels, version metadata, and design commentary in private records. Check the rendered HTML, since its shell can introduce wording absent from the Markdown source.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Recall Rephrase Colour\\" → \\"Read, Recall, Colour\\"\\r\\nThe door\'s heading was hardcoded to a name the product does not have, one line\\r\\nunder a chip reading \\"Read, Recall, Colour\\". \\"Rephrase\\" belongs to a different\\r\\nActivity, Conditional Rephrase. A test asserted the wrong name, which is why\\r\\n2,600 passing tests never caught it.\\r\\n**Lesson:** when a test pins a string, check the string is right, not just that\\r\\nit matches.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Intake only. Nothing comes back out yet…\\" → plain instruction\\r\\nClinical and abstract, and the sentence then told her to read \\"with full\\r\\nintonation\\" while step one is silent reading. The clause that made intonation\\r\\ninternal, \\"hear it naturally in your head\\", had been dropped in an earlier edit.\\r\\n**Lesson:** a word like \\"intake\\" reads as jargon. And when copy is trimmed,\\r\\ncheck what the trimmed clause was load-bearing for.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Three beats through the stimulus\\" → \\"Three steps\\"\\r\\n\\"Beat\\" is the internal code name for one of the four method steps. The glossary\\r\\nsays students see **step**. The interface ten pixels below labelled the same four\\r\\nthings Step 1 to Step 4, so the page used two names for one thing.\\r\\n**Lesson:** never let an implementation name reach the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"22 pieces to read\\" → line removed\\r\\nA **piece** is one chunk, roughly a sentence, and the instructions on that same\\r\\npage use the word that way. The count was of stimuli, so \\"22 pieces\\" read as 22\\r\\nsentences.\\r\\n**Lesson:** a unit word must mean one thing per surface. Check what a word\\r\\nalready means on the page before reusing it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"say it back\\" → \\"tell it back\\", \\"The Read\\" → \\"The Recall\\"\\r\\nThe library door still carried both retired names. \\"The Read\\" was step four\'s\\r\\nformer name and collided with step one, which is why it was renamed.\\r\\n**Lesson:** a rename is not finished until every row carries it. The door was\\r\\nstale for months because it is a different database row from the sections.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"then a fourth\\" → \\"then a fourth step\\"\\r\\nThe noun sat three words back and the reader had to carry it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"once you have got all of it\\" → \\"once you have read all of it\\"\\r\\nAmbiguous between having read it and having remembered it, in the one clause\\r\\nthat triggers step four.\\r\\n**Lesson:** precision matters most where the sentence is an instruction.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The three-fact notice → one fact\\r\\n\\"Nothing is recording yet, the microphone opens on Start, and the section\\r\\nrecords in one take that you cannot pause.\\" Two of those are said again in step\\r\\nfour and in the long version, so she skipped the paragraph.\\r\\n**Lesson:** say it once, in the place it matters.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The method, told twice → the long version collapsed\\r\\nThe page rendered four numbered steps and then the whole written briefing saying\\r\\nthe same thing at length, both open. She read the method twice and could not\\r\\ntell which one to follow.\\r\\n**Lesson:** two explanations of one thing is worse than either alone.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"PT105.S1 · 24 stimuli · about 25 min\\" → removed\\r\\nThe section was already the second line of the title. The clock is at the foot\\r\\nand in the long version. The stimulus count measured the work in a unit she has\\r\\nno feel for.\\r\\n**Lesson:** delete a line that repeats what is above it. Do not reword it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Booking emails: hand over the link, not the pass settings (2026-09-08)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** \\"70 minutes each, and no expiration date, so there\'s no rush to use them.\\"\\r\\n**Keep:** \\"You\'re set up on LSAT U with 10 sessions.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nSession length and expiry are admin fields. The student sees them on the booking\\r\\npage when she picks a time, so putting them in the email is detail she did not ask\\r\\nfor and has to read past to reach the link. The credit count is worth saying because\\r\\nit tells her how much she has. Everything else about the pass stays in the backend.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** in a booking email, say who it\'s for, how many sessions, and the link.\\r\\nStop there.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Drill hub: say what the button does, not what the feature is called (2026-09-09)\\r\\n\\r\\nSix changes on `/classroom/drill`, from the run-3 pass. The hub named its own\\r\\nfeatures, and it assumed the student had rejected something.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Was | Now |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| \\"Start Smart Drill\\" | \\"Practice your weakest types\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Start One Passage\\" | \\"Read one passage\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Start a Smart Drill instead\\" | \\"Or practice your weakest types\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Read one passage instead\\" | \\"Or read one passage now\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Open analytics\\" | \\"See where you lose points\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Open journal\\" | \\"Review your missed questions\\" |\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Smart Drill\\" is the product\'s internal name for the adaptive set. On a button it\\r\\nasks the student to guess what she is about to get. The sentence above the button\\r\\nstill names it, which is where a product name belongs: after the thing has been\\r\\nexplained, not as the explanation.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Instead\\" was the worse one. It tells the student she has just declined the\\r\\nassigned work by reading past it. She has not. \\"Or\\" offers a second path without\\r\\nnarrating a decision she never made.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Open analytics\\" and \\"Open journal\\" name a screen. A student does not want\\r\\nanalytics, she wants to know where the points are going.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe fallback paragraph lost \\"an adaptive set that follows your weakest question\\r\\ntypes\\" for \\"the question types you miss most\\": the same fact, without the product\\r\\nvocabulary.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** a button says what happens next, in the student\'s words. Feature names\\r\\nbelong in the sentence that explains them, never on the control.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Words never to use in student copy\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom the glossary. Internal names may stay in code, never on the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Never | Use instead |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| beat, beats | step |\\r\\n| chunk | piece |\\r\\n| intake | plain instruction |\\r\\n| briefing, phase, tab, card | the thing\'s own name |\\r\\n| say it back | tell it back |\\r\\n| The Read | The Recall |\\r\\n| Recall Rephrase Colour | Read, Recall, Colour |\\r\\n| em dash, en dash | a full stop, or a colon before a list |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Conditional Rephrase: name the student action, not the internal model (2026-09-15)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** “record the route,” “choose the route,” and a map presented as five rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** diagram the statement on paper, choose the matching diagram, and organize\\r\\nthe map into four groups. “Required means right” is a separate takeaway because it\\r\\nexplains placement; it is not a fifth group.\\r\\n\\r\\n“Route” described the system’s representation, not the student’s work. Calling five\\r\\nunlike ideas rules also flattened the teaching structure and made the layout harder to\\r\\nread.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** name the action the student can see herself doing. Do not force a teaching\\r\\nreminder into the same hierarchy as the categories it explains.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: [[teaching-voice]] · [[ASSIGN-AN-ACTIVITY]] · [[STUDENT-PLATFORM-IDENTITY-MAP]]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Session recaps: write a reusable reference, not a play-by-play (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** an opener saying the student got two questions right, followed by a summary of\\r\\nwhich answer they chose and how they explained it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** the concept the student needs tomorrow, a visual model of the reasoning, a\\r\\nshort diagnostic check, and the exact practice they need to complete.\\r\\n\\r\\nA student will not remember an isolated answer-choice exchange a day later, and the\\r\\nexchange is not useful unless it demonstrates a reusable rule. A recap should read like\\r\\na compact study note, not a record of the conversation. Name a question only when the\\r\\ndetails make the concept easier to apply. Praise belongs only when it identifies a\\r\\ndurable skill or meaningful change, not merely that an answer was correct.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** organize the recap around what the student can use next. Lead with the\\r\\nconcept, show the logic clearly, and finish with tangible practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A rule is not a fact (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** describing a conditional Must Be True lesson as “applying a rule to a fact”\\r\\nwithout checking whether the stimulus supplied a fact at all.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** a fact is an asserted true case or condition. A rule is a general conditional\\r\\nrelationship. It tells you what follows if its trigger is present, but it does not say\\r\\nthat the trigger occurred. Some stimuli contain rules only, so the work is to chain\\r\\nthem. When a fact is present, apply a matching rule or a valid contrapositive.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** label the role of each sentence before explaining the inference. Never\\r\\ninvent a fact to make a rule fire.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A post-session note is lecture material, not an email (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** a greeting, a dated recap heading, references to a question the student is\\r\\nexpected to remember, and a sign-off.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** a concept title, a clear opening distinction, numbered procedures, spaced\\r\\nexamples, and practice that uses the lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** the note should remain useful when the session itself is no longer fresh.\\r\\nIt must teach from the page instead of relying on remembered context.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe concept should not arrive as an abrupt list. Begin with a short explanation of\\r\\nwhy the distinction matters, show the full method at a glance, and then teach each\\r\\npath in the same order. Use tables for comparisons and numbered headings for a\\r\\nprocedure so the student can see the structure before reading the detail.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Define the concept in Joshua\'s language (2026-09-28)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** “Some, most, and all tell you how much of a group fits a description.”\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** “Some means at least one. Most means more than half. All means every one,”\\r\\ngrounded in a concrete set of ten cookies before drawing conclusions from the words.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe cut line is vague and does not sound like Joshua teaching. It makes the reader\\r\\ndecode “fits a description” before learning any of the three words. It also risks\\r\\nblurring the difference between a count that is possible and one that is guaranteed.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** open with the actual distinction or definition Joshua taught. Use a\\r\\nconcrete example to show what each statement proves, then introduce notation and\\r\\nthe inferences. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 2.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': "| **Sufficient** | Enough to establish the necessary condition under this rule; not the only possible way unless stated. | Same anchor, “enough for what?”, present-case explanation, absence leaves the other condition open. | Find the complete sufficient idea and say what it establishes. |\r\n| **Necessary** | Required for the sufficient condition; presence alone does not establish the sufficient condition. | Same anchor, identify what must be there, compare requirement with guarantee. | Identify the requirement; reject an unsupported reverse with a reason. |\r\n| **Translation and diagramming** | The diagram preserves the whole relationship, including conditions and qualifications. | Ordinary meaning, indicator, complete ideas, placement, English readback. Introduce later operations when their prerequisites are taught. | Construct a diagram in meaningful steps and compare it with the whole sentence. |\r\n| **Group 1** | Joshua's Group 1 expressions identify the sufficient idea; sentence order can vary. | Teach a simple expression first, reuse meaning across variants, then alter position and scope. | Lesson, guided translation, fresh independent items, explained feedback. |\r\n| **Group 2** | Joshua's Group 2 expressions identify the necessary idea; target and force must be read in context. | Teach the requirement first, compare “if” with “only if,” then develop each expression's target and nuances. | Separate Group 2 practice, then a controlled Group 1/2 comparison when both are learned. |\r\n\r\nThese are curriculum planning rows. Student-facing headings should teach the actual concept, such as **What does this sentence require?** They should not expose “Joshua's assigned starting stage,” internal sequencing metadata, or a difficulty selector. Joshua assigns the appropriate material; the learner works through that assigned lesson and its practice.\r\n\r\n### Give an idea several teachable moments\r\n\r\nThe source rarely handles a significant concept with one definition and one answer. It may need a literal explanation, a familiar example, a variation, a pause, a mistaken interpretation, a correction, and a short practice task.\r\n\r\nFor Group 1, that could become several small lessons: recognizing “if,” locating the complete if-clause, reading an if-clause at the end of the sentence, handling a universal expression, and checking that the diagram preserves the meaning. Group 2 deserves its own development. Do not flatten all expressions into a word table and assume coverage.\r\n\r\nKeep micro-assignments possible. A student who needs “only if” should be able to receive that work without repeating every beginner concept. A general assignment can connect the lessons into a full sequence. Beginner, medium, and advanced describe teacher-assigned work; optional timing belongs to medium and advanced practice after accuracy is established.\r\n\r\n### Preserve the real indicator groups\r\n\r\nThe current reviewed opening materials use:\r\n\r\n- **Group 1:** if, when, where, all, the only, every, any.\r\n- **Group 2:** only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires, must.\r\n\r\nSee the [reviewed opening source constraints](../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/notebooklm-opening-assets/sufficient-necessary-infographic-prompt.txt). These lists are current project teaching groups, not a claim that every occurrence of each word mechanically diagrammed in isolation has the same function. The supplied source's two families have different members. Do not merge inventories without checking Joshua's notes and scope.\r\n\r\n“The only” especially requires its complete grammatical target. The only people admitted are members means **Admitted → Member**. Only members are admitted means the same relationship, reached through a different expression. The lesson must show the meaning rather than encourage a next-word shortcut.\r\n\r\n### Preserve Joshua's five steps\r\n\r\nIn [Sandra's 2026-09-28 transcript](../students/sandra/transcript-downloads/2026-09-28-sandra-notes-by-gemini.md), at 00:43:22, Joshua gives this sequence: identify the indicator; label the two ideas; apply the left/right rule; find the contrapositive; say it back in English. This is a summary of his sequence, not a verbatim quotation. At 00:37:08, he stresses understanding and reading the diagram rather than retaining bare letters.\r\n\r\nThe supplied source has four construction steps. Do not quietly replace Joshua's five with those four, or claim a shortened opening exercise has taught all five. In a gradual introduction, teach the operations whose prerequisites have been established and identify the full contrapositive step when that lesson is reached. Keep English readback visible as a meaning check throughout.\r\n\r\n### Carry these improvements forward\r\n\r\n1. **Define before demonstrating.** Start by explaining what a conditional says. Let the example make the definition concrete.\r\n2. **Explain the purpose through a decision.** Show how direction changes the conclusion the learner may draw. Avoid unsupported prevalence statistics.\r\n3. **Keep one relationship stable while changing the view.** Ordinary sentence, case fact, roles, wording variation, and diagram should fit together.\r\n4. **Make each pause a useful thinking task.** Ask for a prediction or explanation, then reveal the reason.\r\n5. **Show a mistake being detected.** A wrong arrow becomes understandable when its English meaning fails the original sentence.\r\n6. **Teach the target before the placement shortcut becomes a habit.** Complete ideas and their qualifications matter.\r\n7. **Explain why the drill exists.** The learner should know what ability they are practicing.\r\n8. **Use fresh practice.** Worked examples teach the method; new items show whether the method transfers.\r\n9. **Give practical objections an answer.** Time, confidence, notation, and when to write deserve specific guidance.\r\n10. **Keep later content in its proper lesson.** Contraposition, chains, Groups 3/4, and quantities receive their own development; a quality reference does not override prerequisites.\r\n\r\n## Accuracy and method boundaries\r\n\r\nWriting that sounds like the source must still preserve correct logic and Joshua's chosen method.\r\n\r\n| Source simplification or context | Boundary for our material |\r\n|---|---|\r\n| “Ignore the conditional” when sufficient is absent or necessary is present. | For this one rule and fact, no new conclusion about the other condition follows. The rule remains available for later facts and chains. Never teach that it disappears. |\r\n| Necessary has to happen “at some point.” | A conditional is not necessarily a time sequence or causal claim. Prefer “has to be true if the sufficient condition is true.” |\r\n| The original and contrapositive are “all you know.” | The central warning is against an unsupported reverse or inverse. Equivalent formulations and other supplied rules still matter. State the immediate inference limits precisely. |\r\n| Required words are described as obligations. | Preserve modality. “Interviewees must bring ID” can assert an obligation, not prove compliance. Diagram the stated requirement unless the facts establish compliance. |\r\n| Indicator usually comes before target. | Read its full grammatical target. Targets can precede the indicator, and familiar words can serve other roles in context. |\r\n| Shorten conditions to one or two letters, never more than four. | Joshua's clear condensed labels govern. Keep negation, scope, qualifiers, and readability. A short label that loses meaning is not an improvement. |\r\n| Try a personalized sufficient guess. | Compare with the whole ordinary meaning and look for a counterexample. A feeling that an arrow is plausible is not enough. |\r\n| Original and contrapositive training. | Explain why a drill requires explicit work and when test-day practice may differ. Do not turn a training requirement into a universal performance rule. |\r\n| “Always” is in a necessary-family teaching list. | Establish the sentence's subject and relationship. It is not a universal instruction to place the next word on the right. |\r\n| Confidence guarantees the diagram is correct. | Confidence can grow from practice, but correctness rests on preserving meaning and valid reasoning. Provide checks. |\r\n| Timing and learning claims are absolute. | Treat them as the author's advice unless independently supported. Give a practical explanation without invented evidence. |\r\n| References to Logic Games, a book, page 148, or named source tools. | Preserve the teaching function only where our course has an actual equivalent. Do not import obsolete or unavailable context. |\r\n| A theme makes a drill memorable. | Keep each sentence logically interpretable and its answer reviewed. Novelty must not obscure the condition or require untaught knowledge. |\r\n\r\nFor the parcel example, three possible cases distinguish the permitted relationship from an invalid reverse:\r\n\r\n- Blue sticker and inspected: fits the rule.\r\n- Inspected without a blue sticker: also fits the rule.\r\n- Blue sticker without inspection: breaks the rule.\r\n\r\nThe rule does not itself say any such parcel exists. Examples of possible cases illustrate consistency, not existence claims. This distinction matters when moving from individual facts to general rules.\r\n\r\n## Calibration examples for different LSAT surfaces\r\n"}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '---\r\ntitle: Teaching Voice\r\ntype: concept\r\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, content]\r\ncreated: 2026-06-10\r\nupdated: 2026-10-02\r\nstatus: active\r\nsubject: Teaching Voice\r\naliases: []\r\n---\r\n\r\n# Teaching Voice\r\n\r\nHow LSAT U sounds, across every surface. This is the canonical voice doc: route all student-facing and brand copy through it. Built from a grill with Joshua (2026-06-13), then tested and enriched against the full corpus of his real correspondence (944 messages he authored, mid-2025 to mid-2026, from the Gmail takeout; 2026-07-18). Claims below marked with a quote are evidenced by that corpus.\r\n\r\nIt replaces the old two-register / 15-named-tools "preserve verbatim" profile (deleted 2026-06-10) and folds in the brand/marketing register the earlier draft was missing.\r\n\r\n**LSAT teaching standard, selected 2026-10-02:** Joshua chose the supplied Conditional Reasoning transcription as the reference for how LSAT material should sound and unfold. Read [LSAT teaching voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) before drafting lessons, notes, explanations, drills, flashcards, quizzes, or instructional feedback. It contains the detailed source analysis, original calibration examples, progression, and review rubric. [Source identity and limits](sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) are preserved separately. This choice develops the teaching-content register below; correspondence and marketing retain their own registers.\r\n\r\n## Core voice (every register)\r\n\r\nThe constant under all three registers below. This is Joshua on the page.\r\n\r\n- **Quiet authority.** State the value and let it land. Never oversell, never beg, never perform enthusiasm.\r\n- **Specific, never generic.** Real scores, the actual skill, the student\'s own history. Praise is a specific observation, not "great job."\r\n- **Honest about the work, gentle about the person.** Be blunt that the LSAT is hard ("expect this to be exhausting and very challenging"). Never be blunt about a student\'s shortcomings. Honesty points at the material; warmth points at the person. When someone slips, nudge gently, never accuse.\r\n- **Light, grounded metaphor, understated in email.** In his real correspondence the recurring image is quiet: "partner with you on your LSAT journey," "getting set up," "foundational." The vivid images (runway, climb, march toward November, an easy door to walk through) belong to authored teaching and brand copy, not to email. Keep metaphor light and grounded; never float off into abstraction.\r\n- **Balanced rhythm.** Air between ideas. Real correspondence is short and single-purpose (median ~280 characters). In teaching, vary sentence and paragraph length with the reasoning: give an explanation room, then let a short sentence settle the consequence. Do not impose email brevity on a lesson or cut its intermediate reasoning.\r\n- **Plain professional diction.** No corporate jargon, no startup hype ("unlock," "crush," "level up," "game-changer"; "crush" appears once in 944 messages). Contractions are the norm, not the exception. Exclamation points are genuine, not performed: a warm message often carries one, occasionally more, especially in openers ("Sorry for such a long delay!"). He is not exclamation-averse.\r\n- **Forward-looking.** End on the next step or what\'s ahead.\r\n- **No em dashes, ever.** Use periods, colons before lists, the middot (·) as a separator, and numbered steps for procedures.\r\n\r\n## Openings, closings, and signature phrases\r\n\r\nThe mechanical fingerprint of his email, from the real corpus. Match these when drafting as Joshua.\r\n\r\n- **Greeting.** "Hi [Name]," is the default (used ~519x). "Hey [Name]," for established/returning students (~229x, warmer). "Hello [Name]," (~105x) and "Dear [Name]," (~18x, formal) for first contact or a more formal note.\r\n- **Sign-off.** "Best," and "Best regards," are the workhorses (~360x / ~237x). "Thanks," when it\'s a favor or logistics (~146x). "Warmly," / "Warm regards," (~54x) for a genuinely warm note. "Talk soon," (~9x) for someone he\'ll see again soon.\r\n- **Signature.** Almost always signed **"Joshua"** (844 of 944 messages). The name is near-universal; include it.\r\n- **Signature phrases (his verbal fingerprint, use naturally, don\'t overuse):** "let me know" (by far the most common, ~390x), "looking forward," "I hope" / "hope all is well" / "hope you," "no worries at all," "happy to" / "more than happy to," "just wanted to make sure," "make sure," "no rush," "of course."\r\n- **Punctuation he actually uses:** semicolons (correctly, ~41x); ellipses essentially never. (For em-dashes, the hard rule in Core voice governs: never.)\r\n\r\n## The apology / accountability move\r\n\r\nOne of his most frequent and characteristic behaviors, and worth naming on its own. When he is late to reply, or missed or mishandled something, he opens by owning it plainly, often with a brief honest reason, then pivots straight to the fix. No over-apologizing, no excuses dressed up.\r\n\r\n> I\'m so sorry. I thought I had let you know ... I clearly didn\'t communicate that as well as I should have.\r\n\r\n> I\'ve been away from work for personal reasons, but I\'m back now and getting caught up.\r\n\r\nThe pattern: own it in the first line, give the real reason in one clause, move to what happens next. Warm, candid, brief.\r\n\r\n## When something on his side broke (approved shape, 2026-09-11)\r\n\r\nFrom the Sandra booking-error reply, edited by Joshua line by line over three drafts and then sent as-is. Use this shape whenever a student hit a bug or a mistake that was his. The sent text is the reference:\r\n\r\n> Hi Sandra,\r\n>\r\n> I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\r\n>\r\n> To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet. The page was showing a few times that the system then refused, and it looks like you landed on those. That is fixed now.\r\n>\r\n> Your link is the same one as before:\r\n> [link]\r\n>\r\n> Pick any time it shows and it will book. If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\r\n>\r\n> If you run into any other issues, let me know.\r\n>\r\n> Once again, my apologies.\r\n>\r\n> Best,\r\n'}]

If a probe restarts, its log resets gives probe restarts → its log resets. The sufficient idea can appear second: its log resets whenever a probe restarts has the same direction.',\r\n      'Preserve the whole subject. The modifier limits which permits receive the guarantee.',\r\n      'An indicator list is a starting aid. We asked if the gate was open is an indirect question, not an if-then rule. When I arrived, the gate was open reports an event. The only paintings exhibited are restored paintings means exhibited painting → restored painting. Read the whole grammatical claim rather than attaching an arrow to an isolated noun.',\r\n      'Use the What Test to identify an indicator target: ask if what, when what, or every what. In A report appears when a rover crosses the ridge, when what points to the rover crossing the ridge. Identify the complete idea, not just the nearest noun. Then check whether the sentence actually promises a result.',\r\n      'Recall and Rephrase practice: put the indicator list aside, recall the Group 1 role, and write the sentence as an if/then. Read your diagram back in ordinary English. Compare with the original wording and explain any correction before trying another sentence.',\r\n      'Optional wording extension: whenever, wherever, anytime, in the event that, and the moment that can introduce the triggering event. Each, everyone who, and everything that can specify a whole group. In the event that a sensor restarts, a new log begins means sensor restarts → new log begins. Preserve the complete event and group restrictions.',\r\n      'Optional wording extension: The archive will open provided that power is restored states restored power → archive opens. Guarantees and ensures can also connect a sufficient idea to its result. Leads to, produces, and results in express that direction only when the sentence makes an exceptionless claim; a usual tendency or mere causal possibility is weaker.',\r\n    ],\r\n    method: ['Identify the conditional indicator and Group 1; use the What Test to find its target.', 'Identify the two complete ideas, including subjects, modifiers and existing negatives.', 'Assign symbols to those complete ideas.', 'Apply the Group 1 translation rule and check the guarantee against the sentence.', 'Read the arrow back in English and compare its meaning with the original.'],\r\n    workedExample: { statements: ['Any parcel collected from the north depot receives a receipt.'], reasoning: ['The trigger is being a parcel collected from that depot.', 'The result is receiving a receipt for that same parcel.'], conclusion: 'Parcel collected from north depot → receives receipt.' },\r\n    guidedExample: question('group-1-guide', 'A checkpoint report appears when a rover crosses the ridge. Before choosing, identify the indicator, name both complete ideas, place the sufficient idea left, and read your diagram back in English. text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/voice-opening-source-draft.md\n+# Opening source rewrite for review\n+\n+Drafted 2026-10-02. App authoring is paused pending the actual Claude plan review. This file is an external, concrete proposal; it does not modify the app.\n+\n+Read in full: `references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md`. Also read the project-owned `.agents/skills/lsat-u-voice/SKILL.md`, `wiki/teaching-voice.md`, and `wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md`.\n+\n+The first two units keep the same original ready/packed rule. They move from definition and purpose to a fact, an unsupported reverse, the two roles, and a useful diagram. Group 1 and Group 2 then explain each core expression individually. The What Test, complete ideas, clear labels, placement, Personalized If/Then and English readback stay visible. Nothing here teaches contraposition, combining rules, Groups 3/4 or quantities. The existing optional wording extensions stay available with their limits.\n+\n+The 62 practice prompts, option text and order, answer keys, and level membership remain as captured in `visual-proof/voice-application/curriculum-before.json`. Feedback is rewritten below. The four guided prompts and their options also remain. All units after Group 2 remain byte-for-byte authored content.\n+\n+Three earlier replacements reused IDs whose HEAD originals have a different meaning. Rename the current replacements to `group-1-a3-v2`, `group-2-m2-v2`, and `group-2-a3-v2`. Keep the exact originals from `visual-proof/voice-application/historical-questions.json` in a historical-only lookup. New practice never receives those originals. This preserves old numeric choices and saved diagrams under the questions they actually answered.\n+\n+The proposed data below is organized by authored fields so application can be narrow and mechanically checked. Unit IDs and titles stay as they are. The feedback arrays contain a hint followed by the reasoning steps; the existing source helper derives `explanation` from those steps. Each explanation gives the reason beside the answer.\n+\n+```json\n+{\n+  \"units\": {\n+    \"orientation\": {\n+      \"goal\": \"Explain what a conditional says, use it with a fact, and say what the information leaves open.\",\n+      \"teaching\": [\n+        \"A conditional tells us that if one condition is true, another has to be true too. A condition is something we can say about a situation, such as an order being marked ready or its having been packed. The conditional connects those two ideas. It doesn't tell us that either one has happened.\",\n+        \"Why learn this? A conclusion on the LSAT can depend on which way that connection runs. If you know what the rule promises, you can explain why a conclusion follows. If an answer uses the rule in the other direction, you can see the missing connection instead of accepting a conclusion that sounds reasonable.\",\n+        \"Let's use one rule: Every order marked ready has been packed. Take that claim as true for this exercise. Every ready order has to be packed. Orders marked ready are usually packed would say something weaker, because usually leaves room for an exception. Our rule doesn't.\",\n+        \"Now we get a fact: Order A is marked ready. What do you know about A? Take a moment to explain your answer before reading on. A has been packed. The rule covers every order marked ready, and the fact puts A in that group. A ready order that hadn't been packed would break the rule.\",\n+        \"Keep the rule and change the fact: Order B has been packed. Must B be marked ready? Try to explain why before reading on. We don't know. The rule told us about ready orders. It didn't say every packed order is marked ready. B could have the ready label or still be waiting for it; either possibility fits what we've been told.\",\n+        \"We can now shorten the relationship to Marked ready → Packed. Read that as: if an order is marked ready, it has been packed. The arrow records the connection we've already understood. It doesn't add a cause, a time sequence, or a fact about a particular order.\",\n+        \"In the practice, separate the rule from the fact about the case. Then ask what that fact lets you conclude. Write the reason as well as the answer. That gives you something specific to compare with the explanation if your choice goes wrong.\"\n+      ],\n+      \"method\": [\n+        \"Read the rule in ordinary English: what does it say has to be true if the first condition is true?\",\n+        \"Check the strength. Every ready order is packed gives a guarantee; usually packed allows an exception.\",\n+        \"Find the separate fact about the case. A rule alone doesn't identify a ready order.\",\n+        \"Compare the whole fact with the condition the rule covers. Order A is marked ready meets our ready-order condition.\",\n+        \"State what follows and why. If the information leaves a question open, show how both possibilities could fit.\"\n+      ],\n+      \"workedExample\": {\n+        \"statements\": [\"Every order marked ready has been packed.\", \"Order A is marked ready.\"],\n+        \"reasoning\": [\"The rule tells us that every ready order has been packed. The fact identifies A as one of those orders.\", \"So A has been packed. We haven't assumed anything about a different order or about what made A ready.\"],\n+        \"conclusion\": \"Order A has been packed.\"\n+      },\n+      \"commonMistake\": {\n+        \"mistake\": \"Reading a rule as though it tells us a particular case has occurred.\",\n+        \"repair\": \"Every ready order is packed tells you what must be true of a ready order. It hasn't named one. Look for a separate fact before making a conclusion about a particular order.\"\n+      }\n+    },\n+    \"sufficient-necessary\": {\n+      \"goal\": \"Explain sufficient as enough and necessary as required, then check which cases fit the same rule.\",\n+      \"teaching\": [\n+        \"Return to our rule: Every order marked ready has been packed. If you know an order is marked ready, that's enough to know it has been packed. You don't need another fact about that order. Being marked ready is the sufficient condition. Sufficient means enough, here enough to establish packing under this rule.\",\n+        \"Packed is the necessary condition. Necessary means required. A ready order has to have been packed, because an unpacked ready order would be an exception to our rule. So the same relationship has two sides: marked ready is enough to establish packed, and packed is required for marked ready.\",\n+        \"Does required mean enough? Suppose an order is packed. Must it be marked ready? We still don't know. Packing meets a requirement for ready status, but the rule hasn't said it settles that status. A packed order waiting for a ready label would fit the rule.\",\n+        \"Now suppose an order isn't marked ready. Does that settle whether it's packed? No. Our rule tells us what ready orders must have. An order without that label could be packed or unpacked. We haven't been given a fact that decides between them.\",\n+        \"Compare all four combinations under this one rule. Ready and packed fits. Not ready and packed fits too. Not ready and not packed also fits. Ready and not packed breaks the guarantee. A case fitting the rule means it is possible under that rule, not that the rule tells us it occurred.\",\n+        \"The names belong to this relationship. Packed is necessary for ready status here. In a different rule, such as every packed order has a tracking number, packed would be sufficient for that tracking number. Ask enough for what, or required for what, rather than giving an idea one permanent label.\",\n+        \"When a sentence feels unclear, try a Personalized If/Then. Read it naturally first. Then imagine you meet the condition you think is sufficient. What does the sentence force you to know? Check that thought against the whole sentence, not just against a feeling that the arrow looks right.\",\n+        \"A recovery code is required for a verified account. If my account is verified, what must it have? A recovery code. Now try the other direction: if I have a recovery code, must my account be verified? The sentence hasn't promised that. This check helps us hear why the requirement belongs on the right.\"\n+      ],\n+      \"method\": [\n+        \"Read the whole sentence in ordinary English before deciding which condition is enough.\",\n+        \"Ask enough for what. Name both ideas completely: marked ready is enough to establish packed under our rule.\",\n+        \"Ask required for what. Packed is required for marked ready; that doesn't make packed enough to establish ready status.\",\n+        \"Check your interpretation with a Personalized If/Then. Try the proposed starting condition, then compare the reverse with the original sentence.\",\n+        \"Test a possible case against the actual guarantee. Keep a case that fits separate from a case the information establishes.\"\n+      ],\n+      \"workedExample\": {\n+        \"statements\": [\"Every order marked ready has been packed.\", \"Order C has been packed but is not marked ready.\"],\n+        \"reasoning\": [\"Packing is required for ready status. The rule hasn't promised ready status for every packed order.\", \"C can therefore be packed without a ready label. That case fits the rule; it doesn't supply a reverse rule.\"],\n+        \"conclusion\": \"Packed without marked ready fits this rule.\"\n+      },\n+      \"commonMistake\": {\n+        \"mistake\": \"Taking a required condition as enough to establish the other condition.\",\n+        \"repair\": \"Try the reverse in English: if an order is packed, it must be marked ready. Did the sentence promise that? It didn't. Keep the requirement, but don't add that guarantee.\"\n+      }\n+    },\n+    \"group-1\": {\n+      \"goal\": \"Find the complete sufficient idea in each Group 1 expression and preserve its meaning in a diagram.\",\n+      \"teaching\": [\n+        \"We've understood enough and required. Now let's see how different words express the sufficient side. Group 1 includes if, when, where, all, the only, every and any in their conditional uses. The words help you find a relationship. You still need to read what the whole sentence says.\",\n+        \"Start with if: If an order is marked ready, it has been packed. If what? The order is marked ready. That's the sufficient idea. Now move the clause: An order has been packed if it is marked ready. The same idea still follows if, and the same guarantee runs from marked ready to packed. The first words on the page aren't always the left side of the diagram.\",\n+        \"When can introduce the event that's enough: When the schedule changes, a notice is printed. Ask when what. The schedule changes. Under this rule, a change guarantees a notice. Whenever makes the same repeated-case meaning explicit. But When I checked the desk, a notice was there reports one occasion; it doesn't promise a notice every time I check.\",\n+        \"Where can make a rule about a location or situation: Where a shelf has a blue label, its books are available to borrow. Which shelves does the rule cover? Shelves with blue labels. At one of those shelves, the books have to be available. Keep the location connection when you read the rule back; a book elsewhere hasn't been covered by this sentence.\",\n+        \"All covers the whole group it names: All cups packed in the gift set have matching saucers. All what? Cups packed in the gift set, not cups generally. Being one of those cups is enough for a matching saucer. A cup outside the gift set may have one too; the sentence hasn't told us. It also hasn't told us that a gift-set cup exists.\",\n+        \"The only needs careful reading: The only desserts on the lunch menu are chilled desserts. The only what? Desserts on the lunch menu. If a dessert is on that menu, it must be chilled. Try chilled → on the lunch menu. That would put every chilled dessert on the menu, which the sentence never promised. The grammatical target is the whole menu description, not the word chilled.\",\n+        \"Every takes the members one at a time but covers them all: Every guest arriving before six receives a seat number. If this guest arrives before six, the guest receives a number. Drop before six and you'd promise a number to guests the sentence doesn't cover. The complete sufficient idea is guest arriving before six.\",\n+        \"Any says that whichever member of the named group you choose, the rule applies: Any ticket bought online includes a receipt. Choose an online ticket and it has a receipt. Choose a ticket bought another way and this rule doesn't settle the receipt question. Bought online stays with ticket in your diagram.\",\n+        \"The What Test keeps you with the complete idea: if what, when what, where what, all what, the only what, every what, or any what? Don't stop at the nearest noun. In A report appears when a rover crosses the ridge, the target is the rover crossing the ridge. That event goes left, even though the sentence begins with the report.\",\n+        \"An indicator can have another job. The curator asked if the painting was insured tells us about a question the curator asked. It gives no guarantee between asking and insurance. Read for a conditional meaning before drawing an arrow.\",\n+        \"To build the diagram, name both complete ideas and give them clear condensed labels. Place the sufficient idea on the left and what it guarantees on the right. Then read the arrow as an if/then. Does that sentence say the same thing as the original? If a label lost a restriction or the arrow added a reverse, repair it before moving on.\",\n+        \"For Recall and Rephrase, put the list aside and try a fresh sentence. Write its if/then meaning, then compare with the original. The point is to keep the relationship when the wording changes, not merely to remember which side a word usually points toward.\",\n+        \"Some other phrases can introduce the same sufficient idea. Whenever, wherever, anytime, in the event that, and the moment that can name an event; each, everyone who, and everything that can name a whole group. In the event that a sensor restarts, a new log begins gives sensor restarts → new log begins. Keep any restrictions that tell you which event or group the sentence means.\",\n+        \"Provided that can work this way too: The archive will open provided that power is restored makes restored power enough for opening under that rule. Guarantees and ensures can name a guaranteed result. Be careful with leads to, produces, and results in. Check whether the full sentence promises the result without an exception or merely describes something that could happen.\"\n+      ],\n+      \"method\": [\n+        \"Read the sentence for a guarantee, then identify the Group 1 expression. Use the What Test to find the whole idea it refers to.\",\n+        \"Name both complete ideas. Keep who or what they concern, as well as restrictions such as bought online or arriving before six.\",\n+        \"Choose clear condensed labels for those ideas. A shorter label must still mean the same thing.\",\n+        \"Place the sufficient idea left and what it guarantees right. Sentence order doesn't decide the arrow's direction.\",\n+        \"Read the diagram as an if/then and compare it with the original. Check the direction and every restriction you kept in the labels.\"\n+      ],\n+      \"workedExample\": {\n+        \"statements\": [\"Any parcel collected from the north depot receives a receipt.\"],\n+        \"reasoning\": [\"Any what? A parcel collected from the north depot. Collection from that depot is part of the sufficient idea, so parcel alone would be too broad.\", \"The rule promises a receipt for that same parcel. Read it back: if a parcel is collected from the north depot, it receives a receipt.\"],\n+        \"conclusion\": \"Parcel collected from north depot → receives receipt.\"\n+      },\n+      \"commonMistake\": {\n+        \"mistake\": \"Putting the first idea left or dropping a restriction from the sufficient idea.\",\n+        \"repair\": \"Ask what the expression refers to, then read your arrow in English. A report appears when a rover crosses the ridge starts with the report, but the crossing is what guarantees it. Keep the whole crossing condition on the left.\"\n+      }\n+    },\n+    \"group-2\": {\n+      \"goal\": \"Find what a Group 2 sentence requires, preserve its complete meaning, and distinguish a requirement from success or compliance.\",\n+      \"teaching\": [\n+        \"Group 2 helps us find the necessary side: what the sentence says is required. Its core expressions are only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires and must in their relevant uses. We'll keep the complete requirement on the right. First, let's hear why it belongs there.\",\n+        \"Compare if with only if. An order is marked ready if it has been packed makes packing enough for ready status. An order is marked ready only if it has been packed says packing is required for ready status. Those aren't the same rule. In the second sentence, a packed order could still be waiting for a ready label. The sentence permits that possibility.\",\n+        \"Only restricts who or what can have the other condition: Only packed orders are marked ready. If an order is marked ready, it must be packed. Only what? Packed orders. Compare The only orders marked ready are packed orders. That Group 1 form reaches the same ready → packed relationship, but its target is orders marked ready. Read the phrase with its grammar rather than treating only and the only as interchangeable labels.\",\n+        \"Only if introduces a condition that has to be met: The display activates only if the battery is charged. If the display activates, its battery must be charged. A charged battery by itself doesn't promise activation. Read the reverse aloud and you'll hear the extra guarantee it adds.\",\n+        \"Only when makes an event or state required: The café serves breakfast only when its kitchen is open. The kitchen has to be open for breakfast service. Open the kitchen in your imagined case. Does this sentence say breakfast is now being served? No. It says what service requires, not that every open kitchen is serving breakfast.\",\n+        \"Only where makes a location condition required: This delivery service runs only where there is road access. Wherever the service runs, road access has to be available there. A road at another location isn't enough. Nor has the sentence promised service to every place with a road.\",\n+        \"Always describes the result that holds throughout the named cases: A registered buoy always broadcasts its identifier. Start with a registered buoy. Under that claim, it broadcasts its identifier. The subject supplies the sufficient idea; broadcasting is the required result. Always belongs in this teaching group, but it doesn't mean the next word automatically goes on the right. Read the complete subject and result.\",\n+        \"Requires tells you what is needed: Borrowing a camera requires a signed form. Requires what? A signed form. Borrowing goes left, having the signed form goes right. Change the wording to A signed form is required for borrowing a camera. The requirement appears first now, but the relationship hasn't changed. A form alone isn't a promise that you can borrow the camera.\",\n+        \"Must can state an obligation: A finalist must attend the interview. What does finalist status establish? The person is required to attend. It doesn't establish that the person actually attends; people can fail to meet an obligation. Write Finalist → Required to attend interview so the diagram preserves that difference. Keep permission words such as may in the same careful way.\",\n+        \"Use the What Test on the full expression, then check with a Personalized If/Then. If my display has activated, what does the battery rule force me to know? Its battery is charged. Now suppose I know only that the battery is charged. The rule still leaves activation open. That comparison checks the arrow against the sentence's ordinary meaning.\",\n+        \"Keep every part of a requirement. An eligible claim may need filing by the deadline through the official portal. Filed by the deadline leaves out the channel; filed through the portal leaves out the deadline. The right direction can't repair a label that has lost half the idea.\",\n+        \"Other requirement expressions include required, prerequisite, necessary, essential, needs, and depends on when the full sentence uses them that way. A seal is essential for pressure retention and Pressure retention depends on a seal both require a seal for pressure retention. Ask what is needed even when that idea appears first.\",\n+        \"Not every expression belongs to one fixed group. Under an explicit catalogue rule, Activation implies a recorded event gives activation → recorded event. Then and will can introduce the result of an if clause. Signals may only describe evidence. Read how certain the full claim is before turning its wording into a guarantee.\",\n+        \"In the fresh practice, explain what is required and what remains open. If your answer treats a requirement as enough, try saying that answer as an if/then. Compare it with the original sentence and point to the extra promise. That's the mistake we're learning to catch.\"\n+      ],\n+      \"method\": [\n+        \"Read the whole conditional claim. Identify the Group 2 expression and ask what it requires or restricts with the What Test.\",\n+        \"Name both complete ideas. Keep restrictions, negatives, permission and obligation in their meaning.\",\n+        \"Choose clear condensed labels.
